I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.

Although, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.

But, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.

And then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.

Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.

But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. \n\nIt’s funny, because I used to think maybe it was just the chair or too much screen time—my daughter keeps telling me I need one of those standing desks—but it feels more like I’m just constantly keyed up inside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular thoughts or worries that you find are running through your mind when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s usually thoughts, not just the tension by itself. I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. Like I’ll start thinking about my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, and then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something important at the office, or if my husband’s taking too long driving home.\n\nAnd then it’ll be church things I said I’d help with, or bills, or some problem that hasn’t even happened yet but I’m already planning for it. I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. It feels like I’m always preparing for some problem that hasn’t even happened yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling… wound up, almost all day. Like my body is bracing for something, even when nothing is happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I feel tense, like I can’t quite settle. And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. \n\nIt’s funny, because I used to think maybe it was just the chair or too much screen time—my daughter keeps telling me I need one of those standing desks—but it feels more like I’m just constantly keyed up inside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular thoughts or worries that you find are running through your mind when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s usually thoughts, not just the tension by itself. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, some… not like everything has fallen apart. I’m still going to work, I still get my reports done, and I show up for my family and church things. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

But it does interfere. At work, reports take me longer because I keep going back and checking them, then checking again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I attach the right file?” and it turns into this whole thing. And at home… well, my husband will say something simple and I answer too sharply, and then later I feel awful and have to apologize. Same with people I care about. I’m not trying to be short with them, I’m just already wound so tight in my head.

I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. It feels like I’m always preparing for some problem that hasn’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless, or wound up, I guess is the word I’d use. That’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like my body is bracing for something almost all day, even when nothing is happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my computer, and it’s not even a dramatic day, just regular work, and I notice I’m all tight inside, like I’m waiting for bad news. Or after dinner, when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I still feel keyed up. I’ll sit down and then think, no, maybe I should get up and do something—but I don’t even know what.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, accompanied by symptoms such as a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I get very uncomfortable sometimes, and I can feel worked up, but the “peaking within minutes” part—I don’t know if I’ve noticed it like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think it’s sudden, but then when I look back, I realize I was already worrying for a while before it got bad. Like at work, if there’s an email I haven’t answered, I can start spiraling, and then I’m checking things, and then I remember something my coworker said last week—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve had that specific sudden attack feeling with all those symptoms. At least not that I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, some… not like everything has fallen apart. I’m still going to work, I still get my reports done, and I show up for my family and church things. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does interfere. At work, reports take me longer because I keep going back and checking them, then checking again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I attach the right file?” and it turns into this whole thing. And at home… well, my husband will say something simple and I answer too sharply, and then later I feel awful and have to apologize. Same with people I care about. I’m not trying to be short with them, I’m just already wound so tight in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s many things, definitely. I wish it were just one thing, because then maybe I could… I don’t know, make a list and handle it.

My mind kind of jumps around. I’ll worry about my sister in Arecibo, then whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home. And then I’ll remember church commitments, or a bill, or something I said to someone that maybe came out wrong—well, that part isn’t really what you asked.

But yes, it’s not one specific thing. It’s like I’m always preparing for problems that haven’t even happened.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling… wound up, almost all day. Like my body is bracing for something, even when nothing is happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I feel tense, like I can’t quite settle. And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. \n\nIt’s funny, because I used to think maybe it was just the chair or too much screen time—my daughter keeps telling me I need one of those standing desks—but it feels more like I’m just constantly keyed up inside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular thoughts or worries that you find are running through your mind when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s usually thoughts, not just the tension by itself. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll start thinking about my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, and then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something important at the office, or if my husband’s taking too long driving home.\n\nAnd then it’ll be church things I said I’d help with, or bills, or some problem that hasn’t even happened yet but I’m already planning for it. I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. It feels like I’m always preparing for some problem that hasn’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless, or wound up, I guess is the word I’d use. I’ll sit down and then think, no, maybe I should get up and do something—but I don’t even know what.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, accompanied by symptoms such as a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I get very uncomfortable sometimes, and I can feel worked up, but the “peaking within minutes” part—I don’t know if I’ve noticed it like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think it’s sudden, but then when I look back, I realize I was already worrying for a while before it got bad. I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way you mean. I do get thoughts that keep coming back, but it feels more like worries than, um… like some separate thought barging in.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling… wound up, almost all day. Like my body is bracing for something, even when nothing is happening. I’ll be sitting at my computer trying to work, and I feel tense, like I can’t quite settle. And then after dinner, when I should be relaxing, it’s still there. \n\nIt’s funny, because I used to think maybe it was just the chair or too much screen time—my daughter keeps telling me I need one of those standing desks—but it feels more like I’m just constantly keyed up inside.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular thoughts or worries that you find are running through your mind when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s usually thoughts, not just the tension by itself. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll start thinking about my sister in Arecibo and whether she’s okay, and then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something important at the office, or if my husband’s taking too long driving home.\n\nAnd then it’ll be church things I said I’d help with, or bills, or some problem that hasn’t even happened yet but I’m already planning for it. I know that sounds like a lot of little things, but it’s like my brain keeps making a list and telling me I need to be ready.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been… not great. I mean, most nights I do fall asleep eventually, so it’s not like I’m lying there until sunrise, but I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning and my mind just starts going. \n\nIt’ll be something like my sister’s next appointment, whether she has everything arranged, and then somehow I’m thinking about a server issue at work—those things always seem to happen at the worst time, you know, and I used to joke that computers know when you’re trying to rest. But then I can’t really get back into a deep sleep. Even if I technically got enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle pain when you're feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I haven’t paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable sometimes, but then I think, well, I sit at a computer all day, and my posture is probably terrible. My daughter is always after me about that, like I’m going to turn into one of those hunched-over ladies from a cartoon.\n\nSo… headaches or muscle pain specifically when I’m tense? I don’t know that I can say for sure. I haven’t really connected it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fearful or apprehensive about the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. It’s not always one big scary future thing, more like my mind keeps hopping ahead to all these possible problems. Like, is my sister in Arecibo going to be okay, did I forget something at work, is my husband taking too long getting home, did I promise something at church and not write it down… and then bills, of course, because those never politely wait their turn.\n\nIt’s like I’m already trying to prepare for things that haven’t even happened yet. I know some of it is probably just normal life stuff, but my brain doesn’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not like suddenly out of nowhere and then it peaks right away. I mean, I get worked up about things, but it’s more like my mind keeps chewing on them, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I remember once at the grocery store I thought I’d left my wallet at home, and for a second I felt that little jolt—then it was just in the wrong pocket, which is ridiculous because I always put it in the same place. My husband says I have “systems,” which sounds nicer than “piles everywhere,” I guess.\n\nBut, no, I don’t know that I’ve had that intense fear within minutes kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably one of the harder parts. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thought sort of opens the door to five others. Like I’ll think, let me just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly half an hour has disappeared.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because I really did mean to just keep working. My daughter says phones are designed to do that to everyone, which may be true, but still… it feels like once the worry starts, I can’t always put it back where it belongs.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel you don't worry at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not many. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not thinking about anything serious, like if I’m cooking or sorting papers—though sorting papers reminds me I still have that folder from church I never labeled.\n\nBut overall, my mind kind of jumps around. It’ll go from my sister in Arecibo, to whether I forgot something at the office, to my husband driving home, to bills, to some church commitment I may or may not have written down. So it’s not like one area is totally calm, exactly. It’s more that there’s usually something my brain decides needs preparing for, even if nothing has actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not like a song stuck in my head, though that happens too—my daughter was playing some old pop song in the car and it stayed with me all day, which is not what you mean, I know.\n\nIt’s more like a worry thought that keeps circling. I’ll tell myself, “Daniela, enough, focus on this report,” and then one little thing turns into five more. I’ll think, maybe I should just check if there’s a message, and then I’m checking again, or replaying what could go wrong, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour. It’s frustrating because I’m trying to ignore it, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything you do or any situation you're in where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free? Hmm… I don’t know that I can say completely. Maybe for a few minutes if I’m really absorbed in something, but then my mind starts hopping again.\n\nIt’ll go from my sister in Arecibo to whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church thing I might have forgotten to write down. And before I know it I’m sort of preparing for problems that haven’t even happened. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. It feels like I’m always preparing for some problem that hasn’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless, or wound up, I guess is the word I’d use. That’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like my body is bracing for something almost all day, even when nothing is happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my computer, and it’s not even a dramatic day, just regular work, and I notice I’m all tight inside, like I’m waiting for bad news. Or after dinner, when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I still feel keyed up. I’ll sit down and then think, no, maybe I should get up and do something—but I don’t even know what.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, accompanied by symptoms such as a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I get very uncomfortable sometimes, and I can feel worked up, but the “peaking within minutes” part—I don’t know if I’ve noticed it like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think it’s sudden, but then when I look back, I realize I was already worrying for a while before it got bad. So no, I don’t think there’s really a situation where it’s just… quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you consider unreasonable or irrational, but that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do know some of them don’t make sense, or at least they’re bigger than the situation. Like I’ll be at work trying to finish a report, and I’ll tell myself, Daniela, enough, just focus. But then one tiny thought pops up—maybe I missed a message, or maybe something will go wrong—and suddenly it turns into five different things.\n\nAnd then I’m checking messages again, or replaying the whole possible problem in my head. Sometimes I look up and half an hour is gone. It feels silly because nothing has even happened, but it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is something that is present most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yes… I think so. Not every single minute, I guess, but it’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for long before it jumps to the next worry.\n\nI’ll be thinking about my sister in Arecibo, then suddenly I’m wondering if I missed something at work, then my husband driving home, then bills, then some church commitment I said yes to and didn’t write down—oh, I used to keep a little notebook for that, actually, but then I started using my phone and that’s another thing, because then I check messages too much.\n\nBut yes, to answer you… the worry is there a lot. It feels like I’m always preparing for some problem that hasn’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… restless, or wound up, I guess is the word I’d use. That’s actually part of why I came in. I feel like my body is bracing for something almost all day, even when nothing is happening.\n\nLike I’ll just be sitting at my computer, and it’s not even a dramatic day, just regular work, and I notice I’m all tight inside, like I’m waiting for bad news. Or after dinner, when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I still feel keyed up. I’ll sit down and then think, no, maybe I should get up and do something—but I don’t even know what.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, accompanied by symptoms such as a racing heart, sweating, or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I get very uncomfortable sometimes, and I can feel worked up, but the “peaking within minutes” part—I don’t know if I’ve noticed it like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think it’s sudden, but then when I look back, I realize I was already worrying for a while before it got bad. Like at work, if there’s an email I haven’t answered, I can start spiraling, and then I’m checking things, and then I remember something my coworker said last week—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’ve had that specific sudden attack feeling with all those symptoms. At least not that I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, some… not like everything has fallen apart. I’m still going to work, I still get my reports done, and I show up for my family and church things. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it does interfere. At work, reports take me longer because I keep going back and checking them, then checking again, and then I’ll think, “Wait, did I attach the right file?” and it turns into this whole thing. And at home… well, my husband will say something simple and I answer too sharply, and then later I feel awful and have to apologize. Same with people I care about. I’m not trying to be short with them, I’m just already wound so tight in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about one specific thing or many different things?\n\nPatient: It’s many things, definitely. I wish it were just one thing, because then maybe I could… I don’t know, make a list and handle it.\n\nMy mind kind of jumps around. I’ll worry about my sister in Arecibo, then whether I missed something at the office, then my husband driving home. And then I’ll remember church commitments, or a bill, or something I said to someone that maybe came out wrong—well, that part isn’t really what you asked.\n\nBut yes, it’s not one specific thing. It’s like I’m always preparing for problems that haven’t even happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intrusive in the way you mean. I do get thoughts that keep coming back, but it feels more like worries than, um… like some separate thought barging in.\n\nLike if I’m trying to make dinner, I’ll suddenly think about whether I forgot to answer someone, and then I’m mentally back at my desk even though I’m standing there with the onions burning—sorry, that’s probably not exactly it.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to suppress strange or unwanted thoughts, really. I mostly try to push worries aside so I can keep doing what I’m doing, but they tend to circle back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
